1: 0–60Introduce the concepts of living and non-living things. Discuss observable traits like needing food, growing, and moving on their own. Create a class chart sorting pictures of a cat, a rock, a tree, and a car.
Students can verbally explain one reason why a cat is living and a rock is not.		micro-g2-unit-1-living-classify-8a84e2b7b4df-b89494a42e9f54abAbstract / no injected context
1: 60–120Show a shiny iron nail and a rusty iron nail. Explain that the color change is evidence of a chemical property reacting to the environment (air/water). Discuss how this is different from just painting the nail.
Students can state that a color change like rust indicates a chemical property.		micro-g2-unit-1-prop-chemical-changes-66f70a7d12cb-385189205a5191b3Abstract / no injected context
1: 120–180Introduce the balance scale. Demonstrate how placing objects in the cups causes the scale to tip. Show that the side that goes down has more mass. Compare an apple and a grape as a class.
Students can verbally identify which side of the balance scale holds the object with more mass.		micro-g2-unit-1-prop-compare-mass-8da35ca70b60-2ab5a35478588cfaAbstract / no injected context
1: 180–240Demonstrate how to use a ruler to measure length in centimeters. Show how to align the zero mark with the edge of the object. Measure a pencil and a book as a class, writing the results with the cm unit.
Students can correctly align the zero mark of a ruler with the edge of an object during guided practice.		micro-g2-unit-1-prop-measure-length-2d78d5149fa2-98faec927f7c1931Abstract / no injected context
1: 240–300Introduce the concept of qualitative properties by showing a wooden block, a plastic cup, and a metal spoon. Guide students to describe how each looks, feels, and smells. Write their descriptive words on the board and explain that these are called properties.
Students can verbally state at least two descriptive words for a given object.		micro-g2-unit-1-prop-qualitative-28da47c6244a-297b26fab2ad07f7Abstract / no injected context
1: 300–360Introduce malleability by flattening a piece of clay or foil, and ductility by showing a copper wire. Explain the definitions and have students repeat the terms while doing hand motions (pressing flat, pulling long).
Students can match the term malleability to flat objects and ductility to wires.		micro-g2-unit-1-prop-malleability-ductility-4d3a917500f8-b2ad9de9b9f5c00cAbstract / no injected context
1: 360–390Build a simple circuit using a 1.5V AA battery, two wires, and a small LED bulb. Leave a gap in the wires. Test a metal paperclip to close the gap (light turns on) and a plastic spoon (light stays off). Introduce the terms conductor and insulator.
Students can define conductor as allowing electricity to pass and insulator as blocking it.		micro-g2-unit-2-classify-conductors-insulators-35a2ddf67d5a-4245eaf0b183c070Abstract / no injected context
1: 390–420Use a strong flashlight in a darkened room. Shine it through a clear glass cup, a piece of wax paper, and a thick book. Ask students to observe what happens to the light and the shadow on the wall behind each object. Introduce transparent, translucent, and opaque.
Students can identify which object makes a dark shadow and which lets light through.		micro-g2-unit-2-classify-light-transmission-f7576073ad0c-bafd9d188e5c34d4Abstract / no injected context
1: 420–450Introduce electrical and non-electrical devices by showing a battery-operated toy car and a wooden push car. Discuss what makes each move. Show pictures of household items and sort them on the board into 'needs electricity' and 'does not need electricity'.
Students can verbally identify which objects require batteries or a plug to function.		micro-g2-unit-2-identify-electrical-devices-7ca64db22e83-2d763b5fdfd52cb1Abstract / no injected context
1: 450–480Show a battery-operated fan turning on (electrical to mechanical) and a wind-up toy moving (potential to kinetic). Discuss how energy doesn't disappear but changes its job. Create a simple anchor chart with arrows showing the changes.
Students can verbally describe that the battery makes the fan blades move.		micro-g2-unit-2-identify-energy-transformations-a1ad5f6018a3-d73b65f3624883b6Abstract / no injected context
1: 480–510In a dark room, shine a flashlight through a glass prism onto a white wall to create a rainbow. Discuss how the white light from the flashlight is actually made of many colors, and the prism breaks them apart.
Students can state that white light is made of many colors.		micro-g2-unit-2-identify-light-decomposition-681909852415-42166356d573a0afAbstract / no injected context
1: 510–540Demonstrate mixing primary paint colors (cyan, magenta, yellow) in clear cups of water. Show how mixing two colors creates a new color (e.g., yellow and cyan make green). Explain that this is how printers make all the colors in a picture.
Students can state that mixing base colors creates new colors.		micro-g2-unit-2-mix-subtractive-colors-2b0cbc067f6b-7299bab47e73688cAbstract / no injected context
1: 540–570Place an ice cube in a sealed plastic bag in a sunny window and another in a dark, cool closet. Ask students to predict what will happen. Check back later to see the melted ice. Discuss how adding heat changes solid to liquid, and conceptually discuss boiling water to make gas.
Students can explain that the sun's heat caused the ice to melt into liquid water.		micro-g2-unit-2-predict-state-changes-848f411d30d4-4d525bb7eb597173Abstract / no injected context
1: 570–600Show large images of local traffic signs (red stop sign) and school emergency exit signs (green). Discuss what the colors mean (red = stop/danger, green = safe/go). Ask students why it is important that everyone agrees on what these colors mean.
Students can identify red as danger/stop and green as safe/go.		micro-g2-unit-2-relate-color-safety-3953b4b00953-e3a552201ccf59cfAbstract / no injected context
1: 600–640Discuss amusement park rides like a slide (tobogán). Ask why sliding from a higher point makes you go faster. Demonstrate dropping a heavy plastic ball into a deep tray of flour from 10 cm and then from 50 cm. Discuss how the higher drop gave the ball more energy, creating a larger crater, just like starting higher on a slide gives more energy.
Students can state that the ball dropped from higher up made a bigger hole because it had more energy.		micro-g2-unit-2-relate-height-impact-ebc9f0c64cbf-958a6776c548c35bAbstract / no injected context
1: 640–680Explain Earth's revolution around the Sun. Place a large yellow ball (Sun) in the center of the room. Walk a smaller globe around the Sun in a large circle, explaining this path is the orbit and takes 365 days (1 year).
Students can define revolution as Earth's path around the Sun and state it takes one year or 365 days.		micro-g2-unit-3-m-rev-orbit-78a3c1fc7fcc-30be10224453b8a7Abstract / no injected context
1: 680–720Use a teacher-preassembled globe or foam-ball model with a blunt paper-tube axis. Demonstrate rotation and point to the imaginary axis. Students do not pierce or assemble the model.
Students can point to the skewer and identify it as the model's axis, and describe that Earth spins on a similar imaginary line.		micro-g2-unit-3-m-rot-axis-e1cd7bccd789-507e3f9c352d5802Abstract / no injected context

1: 1425–1485Relate the activity to local issues like landslides in Nejapa or sedimentation of the Lempa River. Set up three plastic bottle halves: one with bare soil, one with dead leaves, one with live plants. Pour equal amounts of water through each. Ask students to observe the runoff water color and infer why the plant bottle has clear water, protecting the 'hillside'.
Student states that the roots hold the soil, preventing it from washing away.		micro-g2-unit-6-analyze-erosion-model-ea7faf2fb9b9-2c4e11493c6b340dwater-sanitation

1: 1555–1595Bridge the concept of physical risks (like tripping, learned previously) to natural events. Explain that the hazard is the event itself, but the risk (probability of harm) changes based on where the figure stands.
Student can verbally state that the tower is the hazard and standing close is the risk.		micro-g2-unit-6-differentiate-hazard-risk-11ed4563417c-791ea68720e71b14seismic-risk

1: 1640–1680Display a large poster of a messy classroom. Guide students to point out objects that could cause someone to trip, fall, or get hurt. Discuss why each identified object is a risk.

1: 1720–1760Rub a drop of cooking oil and a pinch of ground cinnamon on a student's hand to represent invisible organisms. Have them try to wash it off with just water, then with soap and water. Discuss why soap is needed to break down the oils that trap dirt and germs, justifying the need for proper handwashing.
Student reasons that hands must be washed with soap to remove invisible organisms that spread.		micro-g2-unit-6-justify-handwashing-05af6da8ecef-843618ed0dd36211Abstract / no injected context

1: 1790–1850Explicitly teach the essential qualities of living things: they grow, reproduce, move, and feed. Use a simple story of a puppy growing into a dog, eating food, moving around, and having puppies to illustrate these points.

1: 1850–1910Explain the difference between qualitative (words/senses) and quantitative (numbers/measurements) properties. Create a T-chart on the board and sort examples like red, 10 cm, heavy, and 5 grams as a class.

1: 1910–1950Explain that Earth's orbit actually takes 365 days and about 6 hours. Use 4 blocks, each representing 6 hours. Show that over 4 years, these blocks add up to 24 hours, creating one extra day (leap year).
Students can state that the extra 6 hours each year add up to a full 24-hour day every four years.		micro-g2-unit-3-m-rev-leapyear-1626e2d19653-771af2d029374a9eAbstract / no injected context

1: 2145–2205Use only teacher-supplied picture pairs and fictional before/after records. Match a supplied change in lighting or movement with the response shown in two fictional records. Cite one visible change.

2: 2400–2460Show two images on a worksheet: a shiny metal gate and a rusty metal gate. Ask the student to circle if the material changed due to a physical or chemical property, and write one sentence justifying their choice based on the color change.

2: 2460–2550Students observe a teacher demonstration of baking soda and vinegar creating bubbles. They then look at pictures of cut apples turning brown and melting ice. They discuss in groups and write down which show chemical properties (bubbles, color change) and justify why.

2: 2550–2590Look at a diagram of two identical spheres, one held 20 cm above a cup of water and one held 60 cm above. Predict which sphere will cause more water to spill and explain why using evidence from class experiments.
Student accurately predicts that a higher drop results in more displaced water and justifies it using the concept of mechanical energy.		micro-g2-unit-2-relate-height-impact-ebc9f0c64cbf-958a6776c548c35bAbstract / no injected context
2: 2590–2620Read a short scenario: 'Maria leaves a bowl of ice cubes in the hot sun. What will happen to the ice, and what must she do if she wants to turn that water into a gas?' Write your prediction.

2: 2620–2710Students work in pairs with a basin of water and a plastic block. They drop the block from 20 cm, 40 cm, and 60 cm, observing the splash. 1: 270–330Students walk a specific path marked on the classroom floor (e.g., 3 steps forward, 4 steps right). Students correctly record a larger value for distance than displacement and define distance as the path taken and displacement as the straight-line change in position.		micro-g5-unit-2-differentiate-distance-displacement-f292a70b33ff-5e5c6663701dadbfAbstract / no injected context
1: 330–390Students drop a small ball into a container of sand from three different heights (0.5m, 1m, 1.5m). They measure the depth of the crater formed each time as an indicator of energy. Students record deeper craters for greater heights and conclude that higher elevation means greater gravitational potential energy.		micro-g5-unit-2-compare-gravitational-potential-ec3a0dae878d-3c2248fb83429b3bAbstract / no injected context
1: 390–450Students roll a toy car down a ramp into a lightweight block. They do this from a low release point (slow speed at bottom) and a high release point (fast speed at bottom). Students measure a greater push distance for the faster car and conclude that higher speed results in greater kinetic energy.		micro-g5-unit-2-compare-kinetic-energy-77ce5e47c325-0c421d771efc6517Abstract / no injected context

Local context evidence

Household resource budgets in US dollars

  • The 2025 legislative text recalls adoption of the US dollar as legal tender under the Monetary Integration Law. Prices in activities must be labeled hypothetical unless separately sourced. Primary source · retrieved 2026-09-14

Coffee and maize

  • MAG documents maize production and coffee-growing regions in El Salvador. Primary source · retrieved 2026-09-14

National protected areas

  • The national protected-area document describes the San Salvador volcano protected-area complex. Primary source · retrieved 2026-09-14

Seismic risk and public planning

  • MARN publishes a national report on risks and vulnerabilities. Historical hazard descriptions are not current alerts. Primary source · retrieved 2026-09-14

Salvadoran volcanoes

  • Volcanic landscapes including Santa Ana, San Salvador and San Miguel are documented by the national geological service. Primary source · retrieved 2026-09-14

Water and sanitation

  • ANDA describes drinking-water supply, wastewater collection and treatment as institutional responsibilities. Primary source · retrieved 2026-09-14
